PREFACE 

Is it too much to expect that the elementary school course in arithmetic shall establish in children the power and the habit of adding, subtracting, multiplying, and dividing whole numbers, decimals, and common fractions correctly, reading and solving verbal problems accurately, and thinking quantitatively in the ordinary activities of life? Such aims are indeed beyond current realization in many schools, notwithstanding the large amount of time and effort devoted to the subject. However, in preparing the NEW DAY 

ARITHMETICS, these have been our aims-mastery in fact, rather than incomplete learning and inaccuracy and uncertainty. Lower aims in arithmetic cannot be justified.

The content and the teaching of arithmetic in the elementary school have been subjected to many scientific inquiries in the last few years. The outcomes have contributed much to our knowledge of what to teach, when to teach it, and how to teach it. As authors, we have drawn freely upon this information in so far as it has been checked by classroom use. In the case of the questions remaining for investigation, we have used the procedures which classroom experience indicates are the most effective. 

Your guide in examining this book will naturally be your background of experience in teaching or supervision. We call your attention to the provisions we have made for better teaching and better learning. iii

iv 

Preface 

1. The text is addressed to the pupil in language he can read and understand. He is, therefore, not distracted from arithmetic by difficulties in reading. The vocabulary is one with which he is already familiar. 

2. The teaching and practice material is prepared in terms of both the pupil and the subject. While each topic is introduced and developed in ways which utilize the pupil's present experience and interests, it is also developed systematically toward the more mature arithmetical understandings and skills which will function in his expanding life. 

3. The full explanations and directions provided help the pupil by indicating good ways of learning, and help the teacher by suggesting effective techniques of instruction. 

4. The treatment of each group of topics aims at (1) facility and accuracy in manipulation of numbers, and (2) understanding of number relations, the nature of the process being studied, the kinds of problem situations in which the process is used, and the steps in the process. 

Correct and economical habits of thinking and doing are cultivated from the beginning. 

5. The four processes with whole numbers, decimals, and common fractions are fully developed through their steps of understanding and skill. There is ample provision for a background of meaning in a process, as well as skill in the mechanics. Each step in a process is presented fully, explained simply, practiced intensively with examples or problems, practiced cumulatively from time to time for assimilation, and practiced in timed mixed drills for quick recall.

Preface 

6. The practice material is so organized and distributed as to result in mastery and retention, rather than incomplete learning and forgetting. Gaps in learning are disclosed by the analytical diagnostic tests and bridged by the keyed remedial practice. The practice material in an exercise is so graded as to provide for every pupil the incentive of success and to avoid the discouragement of failure. 

7. Ability to sense and solve problems is gained through consistent practice in associating the common types of number situations with the corresponding processes. The teaching of problems involving subtraction, for instance, along with the teaching of the process of subtraction-thus establishing the association of the type of situation and process-is in direct contrast to the usual artificial and ineffective teaching of problem solving. A wealth of live, interesting, arithmetically effective problem material is provided from the beginning for training in the techniques of reading problems and for practice in fitting the problems into the pupil's experience. 

8. Economy has been foremost in our thinking in making the books. But economy involves more than the pruning of obsolete topics and seldom-used numbers. The greatest waste in arithmetic lies in half learning and in forgetting. 

In so cumulative a subject, a gap is serious. Mastery of a topic before proceeding to a new topic, then, is essential. 

Frequent diagnostic testing, followed by remedial instruction and practice, is inherent in teaching for mastery. 

Hardly less important for economical learning is the accumulation of a set of efficient habits of thinking and doing. 

Provisions in the book for economy include, then, limiting

vi 

Preface the course to the intellectually and socially desirable topics, establishing a firm foundation of basic understandings, cultivating efficient habits of thinking and doing from the first, and using every means to insure mastery of a unit before proceeding to the next unit. 

9. An adequate textbook in arithmetic for use above the third grade must make provision for the wide range of abilities in individuals. In the third grade, all the understandings and skills are so basic that differentiation of learning must be in rate alone. But beyond the third grade there must be an enriched curriculum for those who can profit from it. Our books provide such enrichment. In general, a practice exercise is not only graded from easy to more difficult; it is so organized that every pupil will acquire the essentials of understanding and skill by doing the earlier examples and problems, and the quicker pupils may progress to higher levels by doing the later examples and problems. Three levels are indicated by an unobtrusive device, but the plan provides for each pupil's going as far beyond the essentials as his ability and industry will carry him. 

While invidious comparisons are reduced to a minimum, the teacher has the materials for accomplishing all the purposes of ability grouping.

In preparing this series of arithmetic texts, we have had the benefit of much assistance, especially the advice of teachers, supervisors, superintendents, and specialists in arithmetic, and the generous coöperation of the publishers and their editorial staff. We acknowledge this assistance with hearty thanks.

THE AUTHORS.

CHAPTER I FUNDAMENTAL PROCESSES Reading Number Pictures or Graphs 

It is sometimes easier to understand the facts that numbers tell by looking at a number picture than by reading the numbers alone. In a number picture, or graph, lines or bars are used to stand for numbers. bft 5ft. 4 ft. 3A.... 2ft... 1ft...

Mrs. Walker Jane

Wilma 

Fred

How Tall Are These People? 

Can you tell from this picture how tall Mr. Walker is? 

He is 6 ft. tall because he reaches the 6-foot line. 

Mrs. Walker reaches halfway between 5 ft. and 6 ft. How tall is she? How tall is Jane, the older girl? How tall is 

Wilma? How tall is Fred? 1

Progress in Mastering Multiplication Combinations 

Alice was poor in multiplication. She was uncertain about 24 of the multiplication combinations. She knew them fairly well, but she was not sure of them. She determined to learn them so well that she could always be sure. 

After she studied them each day, she took a speed test in which she wrote the products as fast as she could make her pencil move. The graph shows her scores for eight days. 

She had 12 right the first day. How many did she have right the second day? The third day? The fourth day? 

The fifth day? 

What happened the sixth day? 

Do her scores on the seventh and eighth days show that probably she had mastered the combinations? After that, should she practice them from time to time?

Using Graphs 

3 16 12 

Words Correct 

2 Days 

Progress in Mastering Spelling Demons 

Sixteen words were so hard for Walter to spell that he called them his spelling demons.

He studied these words in his spare time, and practiced writing them. He kept a record of how many of the words he spelled right each day. The graph above shows Walter's progress in mastering his spelling demons. Study this graph and answer the following questions about it: 

1. How many words did he have right the first day? 

2. How many were right the second day? 

3. On which day did he first have all 16 words right? 

4. On which day did he have just of the words right? 

5. On which days were of the words spelled correctly? 

6. What part of the words did Walter have right the first day? 

7. What part did he spell correctly the second day?

GROWTH IN DEPOSITS IN TEN YEARS 

Scale: 1 in.: $100,000. 

In the Bank Window

The money which customers have in a bank is called the bank's deposits. This graph shows how the deposits have increased in the ten years' time. 

What were the deposits in 1922?

Make a table to show the deposits in each year. 

In what two years were the deposits the same as in the preceding year? In what years was there a gain?

If the deposits increased as much in 1932 as in 1931, what would they be in 1932?

Fundamental Processes Very Large Numbers 

(1) A little boy had written the numbers from 1 to 100, and then to 1000, and on and on until he had covered many pages. He had worked hour after hour. When his father asked what he was doing, he replied that he was trying to find the last number. 

Could he count to the last number? Is there a largest number? 

What is the largest number you know? Can you write it? Have you any idea of how long it would take you to count to it? (2) It is estimated that there are 1,748,000,000 people in this world. The ruled spaces below will help you to read the number.

TRILLIONS BILLIONS MILLIONS THOUSANDS HUN-

The number is read " 1 billion, 748 million.”

Practice Study the sentences on page 7 so that you can read the large numbers accurately.

The commas, like the heavy lines above, show how much of the number is billions, how much is millions, how much is thousands, and how much is hundreds, tens, and units.

Reading Large Numbers 

7 1. In a recent year, the live stock of the United States, according to estimates, was worth $5,807,104,000. (How many billion; how many million; how many thousand are there?) 

2. In the same year, the value of manufactured products was $28,422,848,000. 

3. The estimated wealth of the United States was reported to be $320,803,862,000. 

4. The insurance companies report that the United 

States suffered losses amounting to $506,541,000 by fires in one year. 

5. The newspaper reported that the total amount of life insurance policies held by people in the United States was $78,492,142,000.

Find the following numbers in a geography or an encyclopedia, and report them to the class. Write them in sentences. Read the sentences. 

6. The estimated cost of the Great War. 

7. The number of automobiles in this country in a recent year. 

8. The distance from the earth to the sun. 

9. Make a table of the value of the following crops in the 

United States for one year: corn, hay and forage, cotton and cotton-seed, wheat, oats, white potatoes, and apples.

Read the table. 

10. Write each of the numbers of the table, using it in a sentence.

Fundamental Processes 

Could You Hold a Job?

If you were working in a store or bank or office and did your adding, subtracting, multiplying, and dividing correctly only part of the time, how long would you hold your job? 

If you made even one mistake a day which cost your employer money or offended a customer, how long would you hold your job?

If you wrote figures that people could not read, how long would you hold your job?

If you read numbers so carelessly that you copied them wrong, how long would you hold your job?

Diagnostic Tests* 

If you, a sixth grade pupil, failed to learn some parts of addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division in the lower grades, and do not always get correct answers, now is a good time to find just what it is that you cannot do well. 

When you find what causes you trouble, you will know what to study in order to overcome your difficulties. 

That is the purpose of the diagnostic tests and the practice exercises which follow. They are not tests in which you will get scores or marks. That is why the answers are shown. 

If you do not play fair, you cheat only yourself, because you. are taking the tests to find just what your difficulties are. 

When you have finished a test, check your work with the answers, which follow each test. Be ready to report in what sets of examples you made mistakes, get help to overcome your difficulties, and then practice the corresponding sets in the graded practice. 

* NOTE TO THE TEACHER: Consult the Manual for further directions for giving and using the diagnostic tests.

Interesting Reports to Make 

If you do not need to practice addition, prepare one of the following reports. 

1. We do not read telephone numbers like most other numbers. Tell how we read the following telephone numbers: Main 3905, Kenwood 3400, Columbus 0057. How else are telephone numbers written? What does the word before the number mean, as in South 2568?

Graded Practice in Addition 

11 2. When a telegraph operator sends a number by telegraph, he spells it out. Explain. 

3. The houses in cities and towns are usually numbered according to a system. What is the system in your home? 

Where do the numbers begin? Are the even numbers on one side of a street? Which side? A house may be number 

426 and the next house may be 430; why is number 428 skipped? 

4. How does an adding machine work? Who use adding machines? 

5. If machines can add for us, why learn to add?

Reports to Make: Size Numbers of Wearing Apparel 

1. Inquire in a shoe store about the size numbers of shoes. 

Does size 8 mean 8 inches? What does size 6B mean? 

How does a salesman measure feet for shoe sizes? What is the smallest size? The largest size? Is size 7 for men of the same length as size 7 for women? 

2. Report on the size numbers of stockings. 

3. Report on the size numbers of gloves. 

4. Report on the size numbers of shirts. 

5. Report on the size numbers of collars. 

6. Report on the size numbers of hats and caps.

6 lb.

65 miles."

Reading Problems 

25 

Reading Problems to Sense the Situation 

Do you want to solve problems better? Then read better. 

1. Read a problem first to find what it is about. Do not try to remember the numbers until later. Imagine that you are the person in the problem, doing what it tells about. Or, if the problem is not about what somebody is doing, imagine the situation which it describes. 

2. Read the problem a second time, if necessary, to make sure that you sense the entire situation. 

3. Think: What question does the problem ask me to answer? How shall I go about it to find the answer? What shall I do first? What shall I do next? Shall I know the answer then? Or is there something else to do? 

Do not use pencil yet. 

4. Read the problem once more to get the numbers. 

5. Work the problem. Think about the answer. Does it answer the question the problem asks? 

Use the following problems to give you practice in careful reading: 

1. Susan's father bought a victrola for $50. He paid $20 in cash and agreed to pay the remainder in 6 equal payments. How much is he to pay each time? 

2. On Monday Mr. Harris had $672.45 in a bank. During the week he deposited at different times the following amounts: $172.50, $201.30, and $17.82; and during the week he drew out the following amounts: $57.80, $67.43, and $3.15. How much did he have in the bank at the end of the week?

Fundamental Processes 

3. How much should Mrs. Johnson pay for 2 dozen eggs at 36¢ a dozen, 4 loaves of bread at 8¢ a loaf, and 20 pounds of sugar at 7¢ a pound? 

4. Three boys bought a boat for $48. Each boy paid the same amount. After using the boat one summer, the boys sold it for $27. What did using the boat cost each boy? 

5. Mr. Tyler had $300 when he started on an automobile trip. He kept account of his expenses and found at the end of the trip that he had spent $15.85 for a new tire, $8.35 for repairs, $23.87 for gasoline and oil, $35.75 for hotel expenses, and $28.38 for other things. How much money did he have left? 

6. A fruit dealer had 240 peaches to sell. He packed them in small baskets, each containing 10 peaches, and sold them for 28¢ a basket. How much did he receive for the 

240 peaches? 

7. Herbert wishes to save $10 and plans to save 25¢ a day. 

How many days would it take to save the $10? 

8. Dick works 4 hours every Saturday and is paid 16¢ an hour. How many Saturdays must he work in order to earn enough money to buy 32 baby chicks which cost 18¢ each? 

9. Mary wished to buy 100 little cakes for a school party.

She asked for prices at two bakeries. At one bakery the price was 24¢ a dozen. At a second bakery the price was $1.75 a hundred. cheaper? 

Which price was cheaper? How much

Variety of Practice 1 

In this exercise you will not do any new kind of example, but you will have practice in doing many different old kinds. From now on through the book, there will be a set of Variety of Practice exercises to do every few days. These exercises will give you a review of all the steps in all the processes which you have learned. 

The exercises will help you because: 

1. Doing examples of many kinds will make you watchful. 

2. You will learn to recall quickly just what to do in each process. 

3. You will improve in accuracy, speed, and retention. 

Work for 20 minutes. Be accurate. It is better to do correctly every example you try than to do them all and have some wrong. Be neat and orderly. Always prove or check your work.

Solving Fraction Problems 

49 Sensing the Situation in Fraction Problems In thinking out the way to solve a problem containing fractions, it is often difficult to decide what process or processes to use. If you do not see at once whether to add, subtract, multiply, or divide, think of whole numbers instead of fractions in the problem, and the difficulty will quickly disappear.

When you decide how to solve a problem by thinking how you would solve another one like it, you are using analogy in deciding your solution.

How many lengths each yd. long can be cut from 18 yd. of cloth?

In this problem, if you cannot tell quickly whether to add, subtract, multiply, or divide, think of the number 2 or 3 or 4 in place of 4, and you will see that you must divide. 3 18÷ = 18X 3 1 

24, the number of lengths

Fundamental Processes Explaining Fractions 

Study the following exercises so that you can explain them at the board. 

1. Show that, when you speak of of a square, of a sheet of paper, & of a circle, etc., the denominator tells into how many equal parts the thing has been divided, and the numerator tells how many of these parts we are taking. ​2. Show that = §, be- cause an eighth is only as large as a fourth, and so, to make the fractions equal, you must have 2 times as many eighths as fourths. 

3. Show with of a circle, of a circle, of a circle, of a circle, etc., that the larger you make the denominator, the smaller you make the fraction.

Explaining Fractions

6. Show by dividing circles, that if the denominator of the fraction is multiplied by 2, the value of the fraction is divided by 2.

8. Show whether this is true: If you multiply the denominator of a fraction by a number, you divide the value of the fraction by that number.

Cancellation 

59 

Solving Problems by Cancellation 

(1) In 6 days Harry gathered 104 qt. of blackberries. He sold them at 9¢ a quart. How much did he make a day? 

1. What do you do first? 

Find what 104 qt. sold for at 9¢ a quart. 104 × 9 = 936, or $9.36. 

2. What do you do next? 

Find how much he made in a day. $9.366 $1.56.

An easier and shorter way is to indicate all the work, and then cancel. 52 3 

104 × 9 

Putting 6 below the line 

156, or $1.56 means that the quantity above is to be divided by 6. 1 

Do you see that you have to think a problem through to the end before you can indicate all the work? (2) When 6 pencils cost 15¢, what will 20 pencils cost at the same rate? 10 20 X 15 = 50 

The cost of 6 pencils is 15¢. 

The cost of 1 pencil is 15. 

The cost of 20 pencils is 20 X 15¢, or 50¢. 1 

Solve this problem without using cancellation. Which way is easier?

Fractions in Music 

61 

Using Fractions in Music 

(1) Every note in written music has a certain value, called its length, which tells you how long to hold the note when singing or playing it. The whole note is written; the half note is dor; the quarter note isor; the eighth note isor; or; and the sixteenth note is or. 

Tell the value of each of these notes: 

What is their sum? 

How many times as long would you holdas?as? (2) A dot placed after a note lengthens it by one half its regular length. Thus is +1, or 2. 

Thus.is, How long is ↓.? ? Tell the value of each of these notes: 

What is their sum? (3) The four notes between the two bars in (1) form a measure. The four notes in (2) also form a measure. The sum of the notes in each measure is called the length of the measure, as 4, or 3, or 2. The length of each measure in any piece of music is given at the beginning of the selection, as C ("Common") or ; ; ; ; etc. Find the length of the measure in (1); of the measure in (2). 

The rests or pauses are: = 1; 2 = 1; * = 1. Rests must be added to the notes in finding the length of any measure.

Using Fractions in Cooking 

(1) Mary finds in her cookbook that 2 cups of flour, 2 teaspoons of baking powder, ½ teaspoon of salt, 2 tablespoons of lard, and cup of milk will make 12 biscuits. How much of each of these items should she use to make 30 biscuits? 

30 biscuits are 2 times 12 biscuits. times each item of the original receipt. 

Mary would use 21 

Work this out. (2) A receipt for cherry-pie filling calls for 4 cups of cherries, 2 cups of sugar, and cup of flour. Edna has 6 cups of cherries and wants to make as much filling as she can. How much sugar should she use? How much flour should she use? 

6 cups are 1½ times 4 cups. 

Edna uses 1 X 2, or 3 cups of sugar. She uses 1X, or 4 cup of flour.

CHAPTER III DECIMALS What You Already Know 

Tenths and Hundredths: You already know much about decimals. You have used them since you began to write dollars and cents. 

Last year you learned:

To write tenths and hundredths in the decimal way. 

To add and subtract decimals. 

To multiply decimals. 

To divide decimals by whole numbers.

You also learned to change decimal fractions to common fractions. 

Decimal Places: You know that .1 means ;.6 means. ; 9.7 means 917.

There is one decimal place in 9.7, for there is one figure after the decimal point. 

One decimal place indicates tenths. 

You know that .01 means 100; .19 means; 8.25 means 8,25 100 There are two decimal places in 8.25, for there are two figures after the decimal point.

Two decimal places indicate hundredths. 72

Common and Decimal Fractions: Thousandths 

(1) When .

1 inch is divided into 10 equal parts, each part is .01 inch. 

When .01 inch is divided into 10 equal parts, each part is .001 inch. .1 is 10. .01 is Too .001 is 1000 

One decimal place indicates tenths. .7 is 7 tenths.

Two decimal places indicate hundredths. .75 is 75 hundredths. .06 is 6 hundredths.

Three decimal places indicate thousandths. .875 is 875 thousandths. .065 is 65 thousandths. .003 is 3 thousandths. (2) Read this report of an automobile speed and endurance test: 

Car No. 1 ran 15,000 miles at 61.377 miles per hour. 

Car No. 2 ran 5000 miles at 63.695 miles per hour. 

Car No. 3 ran 15,000 miles at 62.709 miles per hour. 

Car No. 4 ran 10,000 miles at 63.302 miles per hour. (3) Change to thousandths. .125 8)1.000 

Express 1 as 1.000. Are they equal? .125

Very Small Numbers 

Can you think of .001 inch? It is small, but there are parts of some machines and instruments which have to be made so exact that they fit within .001 inch. 

Will someone find out and report to the class whether this is true of a good watch? Of an automobile motor?

Although .001 inch is very small, many things are much smaller. They are so small that they can be seen only through a microscope.

Find out and report about some of these very small things, such as bacteria or the cells in our bodies. 

The shortest measurement that has ever yet been made is .000000001 inch. This number is 1000000000 inch (one billionth inch).

Reading and Writing Decimals 

77 

How We Read and Write Decimals 

(1) We use the same plan to write and read decimals as to write and read whole numbers. 

ETC. 

HUNDRED-MILLIONS TEN-MILLIONS MILLIONS HUNDRED-THOUSANDS TEN-THOUSANDS THOUSANDS HUNDREDS TENS UNITS DECIMAL POINT TENTHS 

37 1 69 28 4 2 

HUNDREDTHS THOUSANDTHS TEN-THOUSANDTHS HUNDRED-THOUSANDTHS MILLIONTHS TEN-MILLIONTHS HUNDRED-MILLIONTHS ETC. 

93 20 375 

The decimal .3729 is 3 thousand 7 hundred 29 tenthousandths (because the last figure is in the ten-thousandths' column). 

The decimal 16.280375 is 16 and 280 thousand 375 millionths (because the last figure is in the millionths' column. 

The decimal 9.421 is 9 and 421 hundredths. 

The decimal point is read "and." Do not read " and " in a number except to show where the decimal or common fraction begins. (2) Learn the column names for whole numbers, beginning at units and reading toward the left. 

Learn the column names for decimal numbers, beginning at the decimal point and reading toward the right. 

Notice the difference in spelling between tens and tenths, hundreds and hundredths.

Multiplying Decimals 

(1) The important thing to learn in multiplying decimals is where to put the decimal point in the product. .7 × 3 =

.7 X .3 = 

76 × 3 == 21 or 2.1 

10 x 10 = 100 or .21 .7 X .03 = 10 X 180 

1800 or .021

In each example above, compare the number of decimal places in the product with the number of decimal places in the two factors together. In the third example, a 0 had to be prefixed, or put before, 21 to make 3 decimal places. 

In multiplying decimals, point off in the product as many decimal places as there are in both factors together, prefixing zeros to the product, if necessary, to make the required number of decimal places. (2) Multiply 2.16 by .005. 2.16 .005 .01080 

How many decimal places are there in the two factors? How many places in the product? Does crossing off the final zero change the value of the product?

Never cross off final decimal zeros until you have placed the decimal point in the product. (3) Copy the following products, place the decimal points, prefixing zeros if necessary, and read each product : 1. 87.3 .8 6984

Using Multiplication of Decimals in Problems 

1. The speed of ocean ships is reckoned in knots per hour. A knot is 1.151 miles. When a ship is going at the rate of 22 knots an hour, how many miles an hour is it going? Estimate the answer. Will it be more or less than 

22 miles? Why? Now solve the problem. 

2. Would you be going faster in a ship at 28 knots an hour or in an automobile at 30 miles an hour (see Prob. 1)? 

3. Don's motor boat can go 12.5 miles an hour. At this rate, how many miles will it go in 3.5 hours? 

4. Our car goes 17.5 miles on a gallon of gasoline. How many miles would it go on 8.5 gallons of gasoline? 

5. In a graph, a line 1 inch long stands for 500 people. 

How many people does a line 5.6 inches long stand for?

9. Milk is 1.03 times as heavy as water. A gallon of water weighs 8.36 lb. How much do 5 gallons of milk weigh?

Dividing a Decimal by a Whole Number 

(1) Divide 21.126 by 7. 3.018 7)21.126 

Check this example. (2) Divide 15 by 4 till there is no remainder. 3.75 4)15.00 

Where is the decimal point placed in 15? Explain why the decimal zeros are annexed. Check this example. (3) Divide 1.28 by 32. .04 32)1.28 128 

What is the first figure in the quotient? Why? 

Check this example. (4) Divide 12.5 by 6, carrying the quotient to three decimal places. 2.0831 6)12.500 

Why must decimal zeros be annexed to 12.5? 

How many decimal places must you have in the dividend to have three in the quotient? 

Read the quotient. Check this example.

To divide a decimal by a whole number, first place a decimal point in the quotient directly above the decimal point in the dividend. Then divide as with whole numbers, annexing decimal zeros to the dividend as needed.

Decimals

Dividing a Decimal by a Decimal 

(1) Divide 70.68 by 9.3. 7.6 9.3)70.6,8 65 1 558 558 You know already how to divide a decimal by a whole number. Look at the divisor 9.3. To make 9.3 a whole number, you would have to move the decimal point one place to the right (multiply by 10). Then you would have to move the decimal point in the dividend 70.68 one place to the right (multiply by 10), putting it between 

6 and 8. Put a caret (1) between 6 and 8, and put the decimal point in the quotient directly above the caret. 

Divide as with whole numbers. 

Check this example by multiplying 7.6 by 9.3.

CHECK 

7.6 9.3 228 68 4 

70.6 8 (2) Divide 1.0464 by .096. 10.9 .096)1.046,4 96 86 4 86 4 

As there are three decimal places in the divisor, put the caret three places to the right of the decimal point in the dividend. Put the decimal point in the quotient above the caret.

To divide by a decimal, put a caret in the dividend as many places to the right of the decimal point as there are decimal places in the divisor. Put the decimal point in the quotient directly above the caret. Divide as with whole numbers.

Dividing Decimals 

91 Check the placing of the decimal point by this rule: 

The number of decimal places in the divisor and quotient together equals the number of decimal places in the dividend.

Everyday Problems with Decimals 1. We started to drive to a town 16.5 miles away, but we ran out of gasoline when we had gone only 3.8 miles. 

How many more miles did we have to go? 

2. If I deposit $2.50 in the bank each month, how much will I deposit in the bank in a year?

6. When railroad fare is $.036 a mile, how much will a ticket cost for a distance of 105 miles? 

7. Frank wants to buy a radio set for $35.00. If he can save 35¢ a day, in how many days will he have money enough to buy the set? 

8. My brother worked a week for 75¢ an hour. In the first 5 days he worked 8 hours a day, and on Saturday he worked 4 hours. How much did he earn that week? 

9. At $7.75 a ton, how many tons of coal can I buy for $155? 

10. We use a quart of milk and a pint of milk every day. 

The quart costs 13¢ and the pint costs 7¢. What will be the amount of our milk bill for March? 

11. Our garden plot is 18 ft. long and 16 ft. wide. We want to build a wire fence around it. Find the cost of the wire fence at $.235 a foot.

Decimals and Fractions 

You have learned that decimals are fractions (10, 100, 1000, etc.) expressed by using a decimal point instead of a denominator. Sometimes you can solve a problem more quickly and accurately by using common fractions, sometimes by using decimals. You should know how to change decimals to common fractions and common fractions to decimals. (1) Change .333 to a common fraction. 

As .33 is 33 hundredths, it may be written 333. 100 1 .33/1/ 331 100 33 ÷ 100 = 100 1 1 3 100 3 .33 = 1 (2) Change .375 to a common fraction. .375 = 1000 375 = 3 (3) Change to a decimal of two decimal places only. means 5 ÷ 6. .831 6)5.00

Decimals

Practical Use of Decimals 

1. How many bags will 100 lb. of sugar fill when each bag holds 2.5 lb.? 

2. On a camping trip, Hugh carried the following provisions in his knapsack: 6 packages of chocolate, each weighing .5 lb.; 3 cans of condensed milk, each weighing .38 lb. ; and 4 packages of bacon, each weighing 1.25 lb. Find the total weight of these provisions. 

3. Hugh's father said that while camping they should provide 1.6 lb. of flour for each person each day. Seven people were to go camping and to stay four days. How many pounds of flour should they provide for the camping trip? 

4. Mr. Scott bought 13 tons of coal at $12.75 a ton and paid 60¢ a ton extra to have it put into the bin. Find the total cost. 

5. The distance from Henry's home to his grandfather's farmhouse is 3.9 miles. One day Henry rode there and back on his bicycle three times. How many miles did the three trips make? 

6. Which will cost more, 3.5 lb. of steak at 48¢ a pound, or 3 lb. 6 oz. of steak at 48¢ a pound? How much more will it cost? 

7. When an automobile in a race has gone 6.75 miles in 

4.5 minutes, it has gone at the rate of how many miles an hour? 

8. There are 31.5 gal. in a barrel. How many barrels will it take to hold 756 gal. of oil?

Using Fractions and Decimals 

119 9. On a field of 36.5 acres, a farmer raised 665 bu. of wheat. Find to 1 decimal place the yield per acre. Find the value of the wheat at $1.32 a bushel. 

10. A farmers' association bought a carload of 25 tons of lime at $9.18 per ton. The freight rate was $.16 per hundred pounds. What was the cost of the carload of lime, including freight? 

11. A dealer received a shipment of 160 barrels of sugar, averaging 396 lb. per barrel. The freight rate was 15¢ per hundredweight. What were the freight charges on this shipment?

CHAPTER IV KEEPING ACCOUNT OF MONEY Personal Cash Accounts 

Do you keep a cash account? Does it help you to be thrifty; that is, to manage your money and your buying more carefully and wisely?

A cash account is an account of money received and money paid out. 

Here is the cash account of Dan Rogers for July:

Received 1 

July Cash on hand Allowance 

6 mowing lawn 

10 Work in garden 

7 Work in store 

24 Mowing lawn 

25 Work in store 

30 Work in garden 75 

9 Bathing suit 

13 Movich 250 65 389 

July 2 3.00 Paid 

2 Put in sannge bank 

4 Picnic 60 250 20 

Ice cream 15 

25 Jennis shoes 125 Share in baseball 33 

26 Put in savings bank 300 

31 Balance 301 13 59 150 60 225 100 1359 On the left-hand side are entered (written) the cash on hand when the account is started and all the money received. On the right-hand side is entered the money paid out. Where would you enter money put into a savings bank? Where would you enter money lost? 122

Personal Cash Accounts 

123 Notice that Dan showed what he received each amount of money for and what he spent each amount for. 

At the end of the month, Dan added the amounts on the left-hand side to find the total amount he had received during July. He added the amounts on the right-hand side to find the total amount he had paid out during the month. The difference between these amounts ($3.01) showed how much he had on hand July 31.

We call $3.01 the balance because it makes the two sides of the account equal or balanced. The sum of the balance and the amount paid out equals the total amount received. 

Finding the balance is called balancing the account. 

If Dan kept his accounts correctly, the cash he had left would be the same as his balance. He can check the correctness of his balance by counting his cash. 

A cash account may be balanced at any time. Why is it a good idea to balance an account frequently? Why should you always count your cash when you balance your account?

Budgets 

127 

Budgets 

(1) A budget is a plan for using the money which a person or a family is to receive during a given period of time, usually a year.

Suppose that Tom Bridges receives a monthly allowance of $5, out of which he is to buy all his clothing except his suits and overcoats, and out of which he is to pay for all other personal expenses. As he receives $60 a year, his budget should show how much of this $60 he thinks he should spend in a year for his clothing, such as shoes, stockings, caps, shirts, etc.; how much for school supplies; and how much for amusements and other expenses. These amounts should be estimated in such a way that he will save a certain sum each year. 

For example, Tom might make out his budget for the year in this way:

Clothing. 

School supplies Amusements $30.00 Church $4.80 7.20 6.00 

Other expenses Savings 6.00 6.00

How much money should he set aside each month for clothes? For his savings? How much for each of the other items?

(2) A budget may also be planned by using fractions. So 

Dan Morton, with an annual (yearly) allowance of $60, might estimate his expenses for clothing at of his income of $60, for his school supplies at of his income, his other expenses at of his income, and plan to save the rest of his income. Find how much money this budget allows for clothing, for school supplies, for other expenses, and for savings. 

Do you think you could cut down any of these items and save more than this budget allows for savings? (3) Why is it wise to use a budget?

Practice 1. John plans to save $9 a year. To do this, how much must he save each month on an average? 

2. The income of a family is $1800 a year. One twelfth of it is saved. How much money is saved? 

3. The income of a family is $1500 a year, and of it is spent for food. How much money is spent for food? 

How much is this per month? 

4. A man earns $1200 a year and spends of his income for food, of it for rent, and of it for clothing. How much money does he spend for each of these three items? 

5. A family's annual income is $2400. One fifth of the income is spent for rent, for food, for clothing, for other expenses, and the rest is saved. How much money is spent for each of these four items? How much money is saved? 

6. A man has an annual income of $3000. He spends $600 a year for rent. What part of his income does he spend for rent?

Budgets 

129 7. A family's income is $2000 a year, and $650 of it is spent each year for food. What part of the income is spent for food? 

8. A family's annual income is $2400, and $40 a month is paid for rent. What fractional part of the income is spent for rent? 

9. A boy has a monthly allowance of $6. In a year he spends of his allowance for clothing, for school supplies, for other expenses, and saves the rest. How much money does he spend in a year for his clothing; for his school supplies; and for other expenses? How much money does he save? 

10. A family with an income of $4000 spends of its income for rent, for food, for other expenses, and saves the rest. What part of its income does it save?

529 purchase like the one at the left? What does it show? 

The storekeeper usually makes a duplicate memorandum by having a piece of copying paper between two sales slips when he writes. What use does he make of his copy? What use does the customer make of his copy? 

On the sales slip are shown: 

1. The name and address of the store. 

2. The name and address of the customer. 

3. The date. 

4. A list of the items or articles bought, the number and price of each, and the extension or total cost of each. 

On the sales slip above:

The number of pounds of butter is in the first column. 

The article "butter" is named in the second column. 

The price per pound follows the name of the article.

The extension (cost of 3 lb. butter) is in the column at the right. 

5. The total cost of all the articles. 

6. The word " Paid." Why does the person who receives the money for the store write his initials under the store name?

(4) Here is a bill Herbert sent to Mr. Carson for work done after school and on Saturday: 

Mr Henry Carson to Herbert Wagner, Racine, Wis., May 24, 1930

Dr. 

May 19 2 hv. spading garden @ 357 

1 hr. raking garden @ 25¢ 20 

212 hr. sud sowing @257 

22 21/2 hr. cutting grass @ 35¢ 

23 1/½ hv. watering garden @ 35¢ 

24 3/½ he painting fence @ 35¢ 

Received payment 

May 26, 1930 

Herbert Wagner 70 25 50 88 38 123 394 When Mr. Carson paid the bill, Herbert wrote on it "Received payment " and the date and his name, as shown above, and gave the bill to Mr. Carson to keep. The bill was then a receipted bill.

Why was it necessary for him to write his name in the receipt? 

Herbert might have written "Paid" instead of "Received payment " in the receipt.

Herbert's bill is somewhat different from the dairy bill and the sales slips.

The abbreviation Dr. stands for debtor. It means that the money is due to Herbert.

The dates on which Herbert worked are shown in the first column.

DOLLARS 

Many people pay their bills by check. When they keep their money in a bank, they may write a check, or order for the bank to pay some of their money to the person named in the check.

In this check, Mr. Douglas asks the Bank of Chapel 

Hill, in which he has money, to pay Mr. Stevens $15.85. 

He sends the check to Mr. Stevens, who may take it to the bank and get the money. 

Before Mr. Stevens receives the money, he must indorse the check by writing his name on the back; this is his acknowledgment that he has received the amount of money which is called for on the check. A check which has been indorsed, therefore, may be called a receipt for the payment of a bill. 

Can you think of other reasons why people use checks to pay bills?

The check shown above has been numbered by Mr. 

Douglas. What is the number of the check? Why does 

Mr. Douglas number his checks?

Receipts 

Sometimes a receipt is written, not on the bill or statement, but on another piece of paper. The following is a sample of such a receipt: 

Des Moines, Iowa, Feb. 1930

Received of me. R. X. Wathins 

Seventy-five a mo and 100 Dollars 

For rent of apartment from Feb. 1 to Mar. 1, 1930 75.00 

O.R. Smith

A special receipt like that above should contain: 

1. The place and date. 

2. The name of the one paying the money. 

3. The amount paid and the reason for paying. 

4. The signature of the one to whom the money has been paid.

Compare these four necessary parts of a receipt with the necessary parts of a bill (pages 132-133). Practice

Practice in Determining the Processes to Use

As a first lesson, write out how you would solve each problem, without doing any number work. For example, in 

Problem 1, write: "Multiply 34 by 40. Divide the product by 4." The first word in each sentence should show the process to be used.

As a second lesson, estimate the answers and then solve the problems. Check each answer with your estimate to be sure it is reasonable. 

1. A fruit grower wanted to estimate the yield of his apple orchard. He got 34 pecks of apples from one tree.

At that rate, how many pecks would he get from his 40 trees? How many bushels? 

2. A family uses 2 quarts of milk per day. How many quarts of milk will the family use in September? 

3. Margaret asked at the bakery for 14 doz. rolls. She was charged 30¢. At that rate, what was the cost of the rolls per dozen?

4. Find the cost of 4 lb. of steak at 44¢ a pound. 

5. John's father hired a man to trim the hedges and trees. At the rate of $5.40 for 9 hours' work, how much should he be paid for 3 hours' work? How much for 4 hours' work? 

6. A hat priced at $8 is reduced to $6. What part of the original price is the amount of the reduction? 

7. I paid 75¢ for a remnant of silk yd. long. How much would that be per yard? 

8. After Bob and his father had gone 60 miles, his father said they had gone just of their trip. How long was the whole trip? 

9. Richard's brother worked in a factory for 37½¢ an hour. One day he was told that his pay was raised to 50¢ an hour. Richard figured that his brother would earn $1 more each day if he worked 8 hours a day. Find out whether or not Richard was right. 

10. A wholesale grocer wishes to pack 1200 lb. of raisins in 12-ounce cartons. How many cartons can he fill? 

11. One spring a farmer bought 18 calves at $12.50 each, and at the end of the summer, sold them all for $450. His expenses were $139.50. Find his average profit per calf. 

12. In a race, a car ran 2 mile in 30 seconds. At that rate, in how many seconds would it run á mile? 

13. Late in winter, a clothing merchant offered to sell 

Mr. Malcolm an overcoat for $45, which was a reduction of from the usual price. What was the usual price?

CHAPTER V PERCENTAGE The Meaning of Per Cent 

(1) You know that six hundredths may be written 18 or .06. It may also be written 6%. This is read six per cent, which means six per hundred; that is, six hundredths. "Per cent" means "hundredths." 

Thus 3% (three per cent) means 180 or .03. 25 

25% means or or .25. 75 

75% means 70% or 2 or .75. 

50% means or or .50 or .5. 50 100 121 

121% means oror.121. 100 In business, per cents are simply the usual way of saying "hundredths." Percentage is not a new topic, but just another name for a subject with which you are already familiar. Any decimal may be read as a per cent. .37 is 37%; .02 is 2%; .2 is 20%; .62 is 621%. Thus (2) Read each of the following numbers, first as hundredths, then as per cents: .24 .35 .05 .43 .18 .39 .33

Any per cent may be read as a decimal. Thus 3% is .03; 28% is .28; 37% is .37½. 148

The Meaning of Per Cent 

149 (3) Fractions may also be expressed as per cents, and per cents may be expressed as fractions. Some of the per cents which are used most frequently are equivalents of the fractions which you know best. Thus = 25%; = 50%; = 121%; = 33%. 75%; (4) Here is a table of some commonly used per cents with their decimal and fractional equivalents. Study it carefully so that you can express any of these per cents as decimals or fractions, and can change the fractions or decimals to per cents. This will help you in your later work with per cents.

Finding a Per Cent of a Number 

(1) Dick sold 45% of his 60 chickens. How many did he sell? 60 .45 

3 00

45% means .45.

Using Per Cents in School 

(1) Emily worked 24 examples for her arithmetic lesson. 

25% of them were wrong. How many examples were wrong?

We may solve the problem in either of two ways. Notice the following: (a) 25% 

1(b) 25% = .25 × 24 = 6 24 .25 120 

There were 6 examples wrong. 48 6.00

Which way is shorter, the one using a fraction or the one using a decimal? (2) Donald spelled correctly 55% of the 60 words in a spelling test. How many words did he spell correctly? 

55% = .55 60 .55 300 30 0 33.00

In this problem the work is easier if you express the per cent as a decimal. 

Donald spelled 33 words correctly.

To find a given per cent of a number, multiply the number by the per cent expressed as a decimal or a fraction.

Telling How to Solve Problems 

Answer each of the following questions with a complete sentence: 

1. If you know how many little chickens Dick had and that he lost 10% of them, how do you find how many chickens 

Dick lost? 

2. If you know how much one quarter of a cake is worth, how do you find out how much the whole cake is worth? 

3. If one pound of meat costs 40¢, how do you find the cost of a number of pounds of meat? 

4. If you know the regular price of a pair of skates and that they are selling at a discount of 20%, how do you find how much will be saved in buying the skates at the reduced price? 

5. If you know how many examples there were in a test and that your mark on the test is 80%, how can you tell (without seeing your paper) how many examples you had right? 

6. If you know how many tenths of a cake are left, how do you find what per cent of the cake is left? 

7. If you know how much Mr. James earns each month and that he saved 12% of this salary one month, 18% the next month, and 20% the third month, how do you find how much he saved in the three months? 

8. If you know how many pupils there are in a school and that 45% of them are boys, how do you find how many girls there are in the school?

11. If you know how much a man paid for a house and that he sold it for 25% less than he paid for it, how do you find the selling price? 

12. If you know the amount of a bill and that a 2% discount is allowed for prompt payment, how do you find how much must be paid if the bill is paid promptly? 

Estimating Per Cents 

(1) A real estate dealer sold 24% of a tract of land containing 1200 acres. How many acres did he sell? 

Estimate the answer. .24 is nearly .25, which is . Then of 1200 would be nearly the correct answer. of 1200 = 300. Is 300 acres more or less than the correct answer? Why? 

Work the example. Was the estimate a good check? (2) At one time, the cost of living went up 41% in a single year. Mr. Johnson's living expenses were $1200 the year before. What would they be after the increase? $600. 

Estimate: 41% is less than 50% or 1. of $1200 $1200 + $600 $1800. Is $1800 more or less than the correct answer? 

Find the correct answer, and compare it with the estimate.

Buying Milk

Buying Milk: A School Project 

In the Heights School, the sixth grade keeps the accounts for the milk and crackers which the children have at recess. 

One week, the orders for each day were as follows: 

Kindergarten 18 half-pint bottles 

Grade I 23 half-pint bottles 

Grade II 17 half-pint bottles 

Grade III 12 half-pint bottles 

Grade IV 15 half-pint bottles 

Grade V 11 half-pint bottles 

Grade VI 4 half-pint bottles 

1. How many bottles were ordered each day? How many pints is this? How many quarts? 

2. The milkman charges the school 10¢ a quart for the milk. The children pay the school 3¢ for a half-pint bottle.

How much more do the children pay for each quart than the milkman charges? 

3. Each day, how much more did the school receive for the milk than it paid out for the milk? 

4. With the money that was left after paying the milkman on Monday, how many pounds of graham crackers can be bought at 121¢ per pound? 

5. How many whole boxes of straws at 231 a box can be bought with the money left over on Tuesday and Wednesday? 

6. With the money left each day, how many school days will it take to pay for a 10-pound box of graham crackers costing 12 a pound? 

7. How long will it take to pay for 3 boxes of straws at 25¢? 

8. How long will it take to pay for a 10-pound box of crackers at 12¢ a pound, and a box of straws at 25¢?

Subtracting Measures

Subtracting Measures* 

(1) In the running broad jump, Dick's record is 9 ft. 11 in. and Ned's is 11 ft. 2 in. How much farther can Ned jump than Dick? 11 ft. 2 in. 

9 ft. 11 in. 1 ft. 3 in. 

11 in. is more than 2 in. Think of 11 ft. 2 in. as 

10 ft. 14 in. Subtract inches: 14 - 11 = 3. 

Subtract feet: 10 - 9 = 1. 

Ned can jump 1 ft. 3 in. farther than Dick. 

Check this example. (2) One day Katherine played outdoors in the snow from 

3:35 P.M. to 5 P.M. How long did she play outdoors? 

5 hr. 

3 hr. 35 min. 

1 hr. 25 min. 

3:35 P.M. means 3 hr. 35 min. after noon. 

Think of 5 hr. as 4 hr. 60 min. Why? 

Katherine played outdoors 1 hr. 25 min.

Finish explaining this example, and check it orally. (3) How many years, months, and days are there between 

Oct. 19, 1929, and June 6, 1933? 

1933 yr. 6 mo. 6 da. 

1929 yr. 10 mo. 19 da. 

3 yr. 7 mo. 17 da. 

June is the sixth month and October is the tenth month. Count 

30 days to a month. and 

There are 3 yr. 7 mo. 17 da. between Oct. 19, 1929, June 6, 1933. 

*NOTE TO THE TEACHER: If your course of study requires another method of subtraction, refer to the Manual.

Measuring Surfaces 

(1) The top of a teacher's desk is 3 ft. long and 2 ft. wide. 

Draw on the board a diagram of this desk top. The shape of this is a rectangle.

A rectangle is a figure with four sides and four square corners, or right angles.

A door is a rectangle. Point out some other rectangles in your schoolroom.

Point out some right angles in the schoolroom or in the school yard.

A square is a rectangle with all four sides equal. 

Have you bought any articles which are rectangles in shape?

In making articles, have you used any materials which are rectangles or squares?

Measures 

(2) Divide the rectangle on the board into square feet, as shown in the diagram at the left. 

How many rows of square feet are there? 

How many square feet are there in each row?

How many square feet are there in the rectangle?

What is an easy way of finding how many square feet there are in a rectangle 3 ft. long and 2 ft. wide? 

We say that the area of a rectangle 3 ft. long and 2 ft. wide is 3 X 2 sq. ft. (square feet), or 6 sq. ft. 

To find the area of a rectangle, multiply the length of the rectangle by the width. (3) Guess how many square inches there are in a pane of glass in one of your windows.

Now measure the length of this pane in inches and the width in inches, and find the area in square inches by multiplying. (4) Find the area of the floor of your schoolroom in square feet. (5) What is the area of a rug 4 ft. by 6 ft.? (This means 

4 ft. wide and 6 ft. long.) (6) Find the area of a rectangle 3′ 6″ by 6′ 8″.

The sign' stands for feet and the sign " stands for inches. 

In finding the area of a rectangle, the length and width must both be expressed in inches, or both in feet or yards or rods or miles. 

So change 3'6" and 6′ 8″ to feet. 

3' 6" = 31' 

6' 8" = 62' 

Area = 

31 X 63 sq. ft. sq. ft.

Measuring Volume 

1 in. (1) The block at the left is called an inch cube. It is 1 in. long, 1 in. wide, and 1 in. high. Each edge is 

1 in. long. We say that it contains 

1 cubic inch. (2) The drawing at the right shows the inch cubes of fudge in a pan 5 in. long, 4 in. wide, and 1 in. deep. How many cubes are there? An easy way to find out is to multiply 4 X 5. Do you see why? In one row there are 5 inch cubes; in 4 rows 

4 in. 

5 in. there are 4 X 5 inch cubes. We say the volume of the pan of fudge is 4 X 5, or 20 cubic inches (20 cu. in.). 

2 in. 

4 in. (3) How many cubic inches are there in a box 3 in. wide, 4 in. long, and 2 in. high? (See the diagram at the left.) An easy way to find out is to multiply: 

3 X 4 = 12, the number of cubic inches in the bottom layer 

2 X 12 = 24, the number of cubic inches in 2 layers

The volume of the box is 3 X 4 X 2, or 24 cu. in. 

An easy way to find the volume of a box is to multiply the length by the width, and then multiply this product by the height.

Measuring Volume

Drawing to Scale

A diagram showing the top of a desk 3 ft. long and 2 ft. wide may be drawn in its exact size on the blackboard, but this diagram is too large to be drawn on a sheet of paper. 

To show this desk top, you may draw on a sheet of paper a rectangle 3 in. long and 2 in. wide. This is called drawing to scale. In this drawing, each inch of the drawing represents 1 ft. of the desk. So the scale is 1 in. = 1 ft. (1) Using this same scale of 1 in. 1 ft., draw on the board a figure to show the floor of a room 16 ft. long and 

12 ft. wide. (2) Using a scale of 1 in. 4 ft., draw on the board a figure showing a school yard 32 ft. long by 80 ft. wide. (3) On a scale of 1" 8', how long a line would you make to represent a line 16' long? 24' long? 12' long? 10' long? (4) If 1" 16', how long a line would represent 32'? 64'?

Estimating and Finding a Certain Per Cent of a Number 

(1) Find 24% of 624. 624 .24 24 96 124 8 149.76 

24% is .24. .24 × 624 = 149.76 

You may estimate this answer by multiplying by the nearest fractional equivalent of 24%. 24% is almost of 624 156. Such an estimate as this will often save you from making a bad mistake. Profit and Loss 225 

Profit and Loss (1) If you buy a used bicycle for $15 and sell it for $25, what is your profit? Are you sure it is $10? It may be $10 or it may be less than $10. 

It is likely that you are put to some expense in making the bicycle worth more than you paid. Probably you have to buy some parts to replace worn parts and some enamel to improve its appearance. Your work is worth something. 

These expenses must be added to the cost before you can calculate your profit. (2) A fruit dealer may buy oranges by the case for 42¢ a dozen and sell them at retail for 65¢ a dozen, but his profit is not the increase from 42¢ to 65¢, because the expense of carrying on his business must be considered. He has to pay for the use of his store, for electric light, for some fruit that spoils, and probably for clerk hire. (3) All these costs which a person has in doing business are called overhead. 

What are some overhead costs in a grocery store? drug store? In a gasoline filling station? 

In a (4) If the overhead of the fruit dealer above is 10¢ a dozen, then the total cost of the oranges is 42¢ (the first cost) + 10¢ (the overhead), or 52¢. 

The profit is 65¢ 

52¢, or 13¢. $.42 First cost .10 Overhead $.52 Total cost $.65 Selling price .52 Total cost $.13 Profit

_% -% -% -% (1) John was saving for a baseball glove which was on sale in a store for $2.50. One day he saw that all goods in the store were being sold at a discount of 20%. Do you know what this means? What was the price of the glove after the discount was made? 

A discount is a reduction in price. (2) Discounts are made for different reasons: 

1. Sometimes merchants offer a discount at a special sale in order to sell some goods quickly. 

2. Merchants may offer a discount if the customer pays his bills promptly or before a certain day of the month. 

This discount is called a cash discount. 

3. Wholesale merchants publish catalogues in which they show lists of their goods and the regular prices. These prices are called list prices.

Usually discounts on the list prices are given to retail merchants who buy in large quantities to sell again. 

The reduced price, after the discount has been subtracted, is the net price.

Finding the Per Cent of Discount 

(1) For $8 I buy a tennis racket originally priced at $10. 

What is the per cent of discount? $10 

Original price 

8 = Net price $ 2 Discount 2÷10 == 20% $10-$8 = $2, the amount of the discount $2 is what per cent of the original price? 

To get the per cent of discount, divide $2 by the original price, $10. The discount is 20%.

Always figure the per cent of discount on the original or list price. (2) Name the amount of discount and the per cent of discount on: 

1. An encyclopedia set originally priced at $50 and sold for $40. 

2. A catcher's mitt originally priced at $10 and sold for $5. 

3. A stamp collector's album originally priced at $6 and sold for $4. 

4. A pair of hockey skates originally priced at $8 and sold for $6. 

5. A radio set originally priced at $60 and sold for $45. (3) Look over some store advertisements to see whether the merchant usually tells the per cent or the amount of discount or reduction. Can you tell why?

Interest (1) When Mr. Brown pays $300 for the use of a house for a year, what is the $300 called?

Instead of paying $300 rent for a house, Mr. Brown borrowed money and built a house to live in. He had to pay a certain sum for the use of the money which he borrowed.

The money that Mr. Brown paid for the use of the money he borrowed is called interest. (2) Interest is money paid for the use of money.

The person who borrows money usually pays each year, for the use of the money, a certain per cent of the amount borrowed. The per cent paid for the use of money for one year is called the rate of interest.

So for every $100 Mr. Brown borrowed, he paid $6, or 

6% interest per year. $100 .06 $6.00 Interest 

For $200, Mr. Brown paid $12 per year. 

For $300, he paid per year.

If he had to borrow $5000 at 6% interest, how much interest would he pay each year? (3) Find the interest for one year on each of the following amounts:

Everyday Uses of Percentage 

1. At a special sale, a boy's suit priced at $20 had been marked down 25%. Another suit had been $15 but was marked down 20%. Find the sales price of each suit. 

2. Peggy bought a bicycle for $30 and sold it for 30% less than she paid. How much did she sell it for? 

3. The total cost of a radio outfit to a dealer was $60. 

He sold it for $75. Find his per cent of profit. 

4. Dick increased the average yield of his flock of hens from 120 to 150 eggs a year. Find the per cent of increase. 

5. One week Sarah played 18 sets of tennis and lost only 

6 of them. What per cent did she lose? What per cent did she win? 

6. Eggs were selling for 48¢ a dozen last week and now sell for 36¢ a dozen. Find the per cent of drop in price. 

7. If shoes are bought at $5 and sold at $8 per pair, what is the per cent of increase? 

8. Candy originally priced at 75¢ a pound was marked down to 60¢. Find the per cent of discount. 

9. Mary sold $36 worth of books on which she was to receive a commission of 40%. Find the amount of her commission. 

10. A kind of meat which was selling for 20¢ a pound a year ago is now 32¢ a pound. Find the per cent of increase in the price. 

11. A grower sold $1500 worth of melons through a sales company which charged 7% commission. Find the amount of the commission and the amount returned to the grower.

12. Mr. Blake sold his house for $7200. He paid 5% of this amount as commission to the agent who sold it for him. How much did Mr. Blake receive for the house after he had paid the commission? 

13. A farmer borrowed $600 at 5% interest to buy a small delivery truck. At the end of 1 year, he paid back the loan with interest. How much interest did he pay? How much was this per month? 

14. A discount of 334% was given on all goods in a clothing store. John bought a suit which had been marked $15, an overcoat marked $12, and a pair of shoes marked $4.50. 

Deduct the discount and give the amount of his bill. 

15. An agent collected $3600 worth of rents per month. 

He received 5% commission for collecting. What did his commission amount to in a year? 

16. A salesman sold $75,000 worth of goods in a year.

He received a commission of 10% of his sales, but out of this he had to pay $2350 for expenses. What were his net earnings for the year? 

17. Mr. White built a house that cost $3600. He had saved $2400 and borrowed the rest of the $3600 at 6% interest. How much interest must he pay on this loan in 

1 year? 

18. Henry borrowed $300 at 6% interest to help pay for his education. He paid the money back with interest at the end of 4 years. How much did he have to pay back? 

19. Thirty minutes a day are used for arithmetic. What per cent of the school day of 5 hours is used for that subject?

Problems without Numbers 

Answer the questions in complete sentences. 

1. If you know how much you paid for a certain number of pounds of flour, how do you find the price per pound? 

2. If you know the price of an article, how do you find the cost of a certain number of articles? 

3. If you know the regular price of an article and the per cent of discount at which it is sold, how do you find the selling price? 

4. If the total cost and the per cent of profit are known, how is the selling price found? 

5. If the cost and the per cent of loss are known, how is the selling price found? 

6. If you know a receipt which will make a certain number of crullers, how will you make up a receipt for making three times as many crullers?

7. How do you find what per cent one number is of another? 

8. If you know the number of miles between two cities and the number of hours required by a train to go that distance, how do you find the average speed of the train per hour? 

9. How do you find a per cent of a number? 

10. If the amount paid for a house and the per cent of commission are known, how do you find the proceeds? 

11. How do you balance a cash account? 

12. If you know your father's annual income and how much rent he pays each month, how do you find what per cent of his income he pays for rent? 

13. If the amount of a loan and the rate of interest are known, what other thing must be known before the interest can be found?
